# Upstream defects register

This register lists the defects and questionable behaviours found in the
C++ Akantu library at the reference commit
`35a851780d0b9471d258ed53a003ae98aaa95ea0`, and what the Rust port does
about each one. It has 597 entries. Each entry has a stable id (`UD-…`)
that tests, source comments, the manual and the parity matrix cite. The
numbers were given in the order the entries were found, so they are not
contiguous and an area's entries are spread over the whole range. Two
entries carry a letter (UD-216a, UD-216b): they were split from UD-216 and
now only point to the entries that replaced them.

The report `docs/reports/03-upstream-defects.md` presents the entries with
the largest consequences for users of the C++ library. One CSV row per
entry, with its area, category, status and switch, is in
`docs/reports/data/upstream-defects.csv`, written by
`python3 scripts/docs/upstream_defects_csv.py`;
`python3 scripts/docs/validation_counts.py defects` prints the counts by
category and status. The parity matrix (`docs/FEATURE_PARITY.md`, column
*Discrepancies*) cites the entries feature by feature, and the source
inventories under `docs/inventory/` describe the upstream code they refer
to.

## How to read an entry

The entries are grouped by area of the library, one table per area, in the
order of the report. Within an area they are sorted by id. The columns:

| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| ID | the entry's id |
| Upstream location | file (path from the root of the Akantu repository), function and lines at the reference commit; several places are separated by semicolons. Where a place could not be located, the cell says so. |
| What happens | the defect and the conditions under which a user of the C++ library meets it |
| Cat. | the category (next section); a parenthesis qualifies it |
| In the port | the status (first word, see below), then what the port does by default: corrected, reproduced, refused (with the error it returns) or given a defined result |
| Switch or patch | the compatibility switch that restores upstream's behaviour, and the oracle patch under `oracle/patches/` when the C++ reference used for comparison was patched; `No switch` when there is none |
| Tests | Rust tests (crate, test file, test name), Python tests under `python/tests/` and oracle fixtures under `fixtures/` that show the port's handling; `—` when no test is dedicated to the entry |

Where an entry needs a longer explanation (the specification of a corrected
law, an analysis of when the defect can be observed), it follows the area's
table as a subsection titled with the id.

Line numbers are those of the reference commit. Paths of the port are
relative to the root of this repository; a test named
`akantu-solid` `inelastic_properties::ud038_thermal_state_of_inelastic_laws`
is the function `ud038_thermal_state_of_inelastic_laws` in
`crates/akantu-solid/tests/inelastic_properties.rs`.

## Categories and the policy for each

The categories and the policy are those of decision D-003 in
`docs/DECISIONS.md`. A category describes the C++ behaviour and, through
the policy, the port's default: since decision D-115 an entry whose
upstream behaviour the port keeps is C, and one it corrects is B.

| Category | Meaning | Policy in the port | Entries |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | undefined behaviour: uninitialised memory, out-of-bounds access, use after free, a crash or an abort on valid input | the port implements defined behaviour, usually an error or zero-initialised data. Differential tests compare with an oracle patched to define the behaviour the same way (`oracle/patches/`), with upstream on the inputs where its behaviour is defined, or with analytical results. | 148 |
| B | a clear bug that gives wrong results | the port computes the corrected result by default. Where it is practical, a compatibility switch reproduces upstream's result, and the differential tests replay the oracle fixtures with it. The policy requires an independent scientific review of each correction. | 242 |
| C | a quirk or an API semantic | the port keeps upstream's behaviour by default and documents it; it may add an explicit API for the other behaviour | 177 |
| test infra | a defect of upstream's tests, test driver or CMake test registration; the library itself is not concerned | the port ports the test's intent and says how its version differs | 20 |
| build | upstream does not compile or link in a configuration | the port builds; the oracle uses a patch where one is needed | 3 |
| scope | a whole package that does not compile at the reference commit | ported from its source and its intent | 1 |
| dead code | code that nothing reaches | not ported | 1 |
| — | withdrawn, or no defect after analysis | the cell of the port says why | 5 |

A category is counted by its first letter. 38 entries also name a second
category in the same cell, for example `A/B` (an uninitialised read whose
value also makes a result wrong) or `C (B for the stiffness flag)` (one
part of the entry is a bug); `validation_counts.py` prints them as `A+`,
`B+` and `C+`. The parenthesis qualifies the entry; the recurring ones:

- `latent`: no path of upstream's library reaches the defect through its
  public API and its examples at the reference commit, but a user's code or
  a later change could;
- `test defect`: upstream's library is right and one of its tests is
  wrong; the port replays the corrected test;
- `not a defect`: the behaviour is intended; it is listed because a user
  could take it for one;
- `documented difference`: a deliberate, documented deviation of the port's
  API or file format where upstream has no numerical behaviour to keep and
  no bug to correct (it counts with C);
- `environmental reproducibility`: results depend on the machine, the
  compiler or a library version, for example the Gmsh build that meshes a
  `.geo` file.

## Status

The first word of the *In the port* cell is the entry's status.

| Status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Implemented | the handling the cell describes is in the port's code, with the tests named |
| Documented | the port keeps upstream's behaviour, or there is nothing in the port to change; the behaviour is described in the manual or in this register |
| Decided | the handling is fixed but not in the port's code yet; the cell says what the port does meanwhile |
| Withdrawn | the entry turned out not to be a defect; it is kept so that its id stays valid |
| Superseded | another entry, named in the cell, replaces this one |
| Registered | the entry was split and registered under the ids named in the cell |
| Not ported | the affected upstream code has no counterpart in the port |

## Compatibility switches

Every switch is in its corrected position by default, and setting it
restores upstream's behaviour. Most switches are boolean fields that
default to `false`; a few option fields default to `true` and restore
upstream with `false` (for example
`LawOptions::material_axes_as_directions`), and some are enums with an
`Upstream…` variant. Switches are grouped per area in structs whose name ends in
`UpstreamCompat` or `Compat` (`CohesiveUpstreamCompat`,
`ContactUpstreamCompat`, `PhaseFieldUpstreamCompat`, `SmmUpstreamCompat`,
`StructuralUpstreamCompat`, `StructuralModelUpstreamCompat`,
`TasnUpstreamCompat`, `DiffusionUpstreamCompat`,
`EmbeddedUpstreamCompat`, `SolidContactCompat`,
`CouplerSolidPhaseFieldCompat`, `PartitionCompat`, `SparseSolverCompat`,
and `akantu::solver::UpstreamCompat` for the solvers). Each field is named
after the upstream behaviour it restores, for example
`CohesiveUpstreamCompat::tangent_rotation_2d_in_3d`; the constructors
`upstream()`, `all()` or the constant `UPSTREAM` turn on every field of a
struct. The material laws take `LawOptions` fields whose `Upstream…`
variant restores upstream (`LawOptions::upstream()` sets all of them);
option structs such as `ExtraLawOptions`, `NonLocalOptions`,
`ReadOptions` and `SegmentIntersectorOptions` work the same way. A
few switches are methods or options, such as
`NonLinearSolverNewtonRaphson::set_legacy_hooks(true)`,
`DumperParaview::set_compat_layout(true)` or
`WriteOptions::upstream_precision()`. The Python bindings expose
`setUpstreamCompat(True)` on the contact and phase-field models and on the
couplers.

The manual lists the switches with upstream's behaviour and the port's
default in `docs/manual/src/differences.md` (section "How upstream defects
are handled") and in the model chapters it links to. Tests that replay an
oracle fixture with a switch on, or against a patched oracle, cite the
entry's id.

## Oracle patches

The C++ oracle is upstream built from the reference commit
(`docs/ORACLE_BUILD.md`). The patches under `oracle/patches/` change it
only where a comparison needs it. The patches in `variants/` are not part
of the regular oracle build: each gives a separate oracle build whose
fixtures the port compares with on the inputs concerned.

| Patch | Entries | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `0001-tests-dereference-mpiexec-max-numprocs.patch` | UD-015 | registers the PARALLEL tests with their rank counts |
| `variants/ud001-contact-zero-init.patch` | UD-001 | zero-initialises the contact vectors, for the friction fixtures |
| `variants/ud031-anisotropic-damage-zero-init.patch` | UD-031 | zero-initialises the anisotropic damage law's work matrix |
| `variants/ud814-eig-noconvergence-zero-init.patch` | UD-814 | defines the eigenpairs when Eigen's solver does not converge |
| `geometry/0001-mesh-sphere-intersector-compile-fixes.patch` | UD-409 | makes `MeshSphereIntersector` compile |
| `geometry/0002-mesh-sphere-intersector-zero-fill.patch` | UD-414 | removes its out-of-bounds write |
| `embedded/0001-model-options-named-argument-arity.patch` | UD-073 | makes `model_options.hh` compile with the embedded package on |
| `embedded/0002` to `0004`, `0007`, `0008` | UD-587 | API adaptations and test fixes of the embedded package |
| `embedded/0005-reinforcement-pre-stress-internal-ud588.patch` | UD-588 | registers `pre_stress` once, as the released v5.0.0 did |
| `embedded/0006-reinforcement-one-dimensional-law-ud589.patch` | UD-589 | gives the reinforcement its one-dimensional law back, as the released v5.0.0 did |

The SLS dissipated energy uses a matrix product followed by `.sum()` instead of a contraction. | B | Implemented: corrected by default, the Maxwell recurrence is stored in `voigt_sigma_v` and `voigt_epsilon_v` computed from it (the port's reading of the intent), and the SLS dissipation is a contraction. ### UD-224: the zero-strength interface

Giving these laws σc, as bilinear does, would not help: the Camacho–Ortiz
law has no elastic branch, so the traction would jump from the insertion
stress (0) to about σc at δ = 0⁺, an infinitely stiff interface. In
upstream's `implicit_static_linear_triangle_6` the interface needs 913
Newton iterations and the reaction is about 1e-6, the size of the final
residual, with σc,eff = 0 recorded.

The port's check runs inside the insertion transaction, after the
topology change and before the commit: the model's selector chain chooses
the law of every new cohesive element on the staged mesh exactly as the
later `ElementsAdded` dispatch will, and that decision is staged so a
custom selector cannot assign another law afterwards. A refusal rolls the
transaction back and restores the law-index map and the insertion flags.
A selector that gives the facets a law of the linear family and their
cohesive elements a law with strength is accepted.

### UD-803: dissipated energy of under-resolved runs

The extrinsic linear law holds the insertion traction `t_ins` while the
damage is 0 and drops to 0 once `δmax ≥ δc,eff`; `t_ins` has a compressive
normal part and a shear part that need not align with the later sliding.
With κ = 1 the law (free energy `ψ = ½ K(d) δ²`, `K = σc,eff (1 − d)/δmax`)
dissipates exactly `D = ½ σc,eff min(δmax, δc,eff) ≥ 0` per point, and
`Gc = ½ σc,eff δc,eff = ½ σc δc` for a broken point. The port's check
`assert_exact_dissipation` verifies per point that the damage is
`min(δmax/δc,eff, 1)`, `D ≥ 0` and `½ σc,eff δc,eff = ½ σc δc`, and in
total `∫ Gc [broken] ≤ ∫ D ≤ ∫ Gc`. After 1100 steps of `scalability_test`,
18 of 21 points are broken, the bookkept `Ed` is 2.7603 and `D` is 5.4672,
and one point has negative bookkeeping.

### UD-890: what is known about the ghost boundary corruption

Observed on the strict oracle with MUMPS, the frozen h = 0.12 cube,
`AKA_SEED = 1`, the exported Scotch partition and 1,500 steps, with the
same problem at every rank count: between steps 800 and 899 (the first
insertions are at steps 710 to 799) the ghost `_triangle_3` elements 23,
24, 38, 41 of "side left" and 68, 78 of "side right" on rank 0, six
different boundary triangles at step 800, all carry the node set (2261,
2548, 2963) at step 899; on rank 1 the ghost elements 12 and 13 of "side
left" carry (2134, 3252, 3337). The serial run and the local elements of
both ranks show only the legitimate update of a doubled node.

The statement in the source that writes boundary connectivities after an
insertion is the loop in `Mesh::sendEvent<NewNodesEvent>`
(`mesh.cc:139-142`): for each boundary element `el` of each ghost type it
sets `conn = mesh_facets->connectivities(type, ghost_type)[mesh_to_mesh_facet(el).element]`.
It indexes the facet block of the boundary element's own ghost type with
the element number of the mapped facet and does not use the mapped
facet's ghost type. A ghost boundary element whose `mesh_to_mesh_facet`
entry is stale, or names a facet of the other ghost type, therefore
receives another facet's nodes. Whether this is the path that produced the
observed corruption was not established: no instrumented oracle run
traced the write.

### UD-1280: external forces written outside the Neumann functions

Before each recorded application and each move, the store compares the
external force with its own view of it (decision D-108). A node that
another path set to zero is a reset: its recorded contributions there are
forgotten, so the "zero, then apply again" loop of upstream's examples
records the last application. A node that another path changed to any
other value is taken as carrying an added force, which stays with the
original copy while the node's Neumann records move. Rescaling or
replacing Neumann loads by writing the external force array (a load ramp
`f ← λ f`, hand-computed values) on a model that may insert at loaded
faces is not supported by the corrected default: zero the array and apply
the loads again, or use the switch.

With elongated facets next to the contact surface (side cells much longer than the surface cells) each cell holds many master nodes and the local search degenerates towards `O(slaves × masters)`; the results are upstream's. Measured on the port: 1.2 s per search with 2000:1 side cells against 56 ms with square cells, same surfaces (40 k / 30 k segments). | C | Implemented: upstream's spacing is the default. `GridSpacing::ContactSurface` (opt-in, `ContactDetector::grid_spacing`, `ContactMechanicsModel::set_grid_spacing`) takes the contact-surface facets only; the 3^d cells around a slave then cover another region, so the closest master node found can differ when it lies farther than one cell. `maximum_bounding_box` and the minimal distance are unchanged. | No switch (the default is upstream's) | `akantu-contact` unit test `detector::tests::grid_spacing_from_the_contact_surface` |

### UD-368: the consistent normal tangent

With `N = Aᵀn`, `N̄_α = A_αᵀn`, `T_α = Aᵀa_α` (natural tangents `a_α`),
`m = a·a`, `κ_αβ = n·∂²_αβ x` and `H = m + g κ`, the port's normal tangent is

`K = A_s [p′ N Nᵀ + p (T H⁻¹ N̄ᵀ + N̄ H⁻¹ Tᵀ − g N̄ H⁻¹ N̄ᵀ + T m⁻¹ κ H⁻¹ Tᵀ)]`,

symmetric, with the nodal area `A_s` frozen. It replaces the UD-008
correction on the default path.

### UD-369: the consistent friction tangent

The tangent follows from the frozen history (`t^p`, `τ^p`, `ξ_p`, previous
facet) and frozen nodal area:

- `δξ = H⁻¹ (T − g N̄) · δu`;
- `δτ_α = s_α (I − τ_α τ_αᵀ) δa_α / ‖a_α‖`;
- `δt^tr` from the transported traction and `δΔx`;
- in slip, `δt = μ [δp_N t^tr/q + p_N δt^tr/q − p_N t^tr δq/q²]` with
  `δq = (t^tr m̂⁻¹ δt^tr + ½ t^tr δm̂⁻¹ t^tr)/q`;
- the transport term `δ(Aᵀτ_α) = Aᵀ δτ_α − Σ_γ A_γᵀ τ_α δξ^γ`.

When the slave changed facet on a deformable master, `δΔx` also depends on
the previous facet's nodes outside the element; their columns are computed
with the element block and assembled as a rectangular block. Without them
an assembled `K_T` is 21 to 25 % off in stick and 2 to 4 % in 3-D slip,
which element-level finite differences cannot see.

Element-level central differences (h = 1e-7) of the port's own force over
36 configurations (2-D and 3-D, stick and slip, flat, tilted and curved
masters, segment, quadrangle and triangle facets, both pressure laws) agree
to 1.7e-9, also with the UD-365 switch. At the assembled level, 48
configurations including facet changes on deformable masters agree to
9.1e-10, and with a non-zero traction history to 8.6e-10. Newton's order
is 2.2 in 2-D and 2.0 in 3-D with the nodal area frozen.

### UD-1291: search order of the static and implicit couplers

Step sequence with Newton and the `residual` criterion. Upstream:
`predictor`, residual on the previous contact set, test at iteration 0
(a converged step ends on the stale state), then the loop (`K`, solve,
corrector with search, residual, test). Port default: `predictor` (search
owed), search on the predicted positions, residual, test at iteration 0 on
the configuration's own contact state, then the same loop. The search
before the first residual runs after the integration scheme's predictor,
as the time-step solver calls the callback's `predictor` first in both
codes. It keeps the friction history (committed once per converged step
under UD-374's default, advanced at each corrector under UD-374's switch).

Of the five static and implicit coupler fixtures only
`cohesive_implicit_dynamic_qs` needs the switch: its Newmark predictor
moves the crack faces, so the corrected order departs at the first residual
of step 1. `cohesive_static`, `contact_static_hertz`,
`contact_static_friction` and the `contact_explicit_static` comparisons are
bitwise in both orders (their predictor moves no contact node), and
`cohesive_implicit_dynamic_dt0p01` is bitwise in both orders because its
moved crack faces do not penetrate.

Closed forms (`ud1291_search_order.rs`, two blocks through
`CouplerSolidContact`): a rigid translation into contact converges at
iteration 0 with penetration 2⁻¹⁰ and no contact force under the switch,
and with the exact `ε_n g A` by default; with the master free,
`p = g/(1/ε_n + 1/E)` within 1e-10 by default. A ten-step load path is
bitwise equal in both orders up to step 5 and differs from step 6, the
first step whose first residual converges under the switch. The crack-shear
closed forms of `cohesive_contact_friction.rs` hold by default without the
small compression increments that upstream's order needs. What the
correction does not remove is UD-1293.

### UD-059: the lumped rotary inertia of the port

The coefficients are the HRZ scaling of the consistent Hermite diagonal:
`J_b = m L²/78` (the consistent `4L² ρAL/420` scaled by `420/312`) for each
bending rotation and `J_t = ρ I_p L/2` (the consistent `ρ I_p L/3` scaled
the same way) for the torsion. In 2-D the rotation of both nodes gets
`J_b`.

`LumpedRotaryInertia::Block3x3` (default) puts in 3-D the nodal block
`Jₙ = Σ_e Tₑᵀ diag(J_t, J_b, J_b) Tₑ` on `θx, θy, θz`, where `Tₑ` is the
element's global-to-member rotation used by the FE engine for the stiffness
and the consistent mass. It is objective (blocks map to `Q J Qᵀ` under a
rigid rotation; measured to 3.4 ε, lumped spectrum unchanged to 2.0e-12)
and exact HRZ in every member frame: the torsional fundamental of a
clamped–free shaft is the lumped-chain value `(4N/π) sin(π/(4N))` of the
exact one in every orientation (steel shaft `r = 0.02`, `L = 1.5`: −1.1384 %,
−0.2853 %, −0.0714 % at 3, 6, 12 elements). The off-diagonal entries are
block couplings of the lumped `M`: the residual uses the block product, the
explicit lumped solve factorises each node's free sub-block (`LDLᵀ`; a
blocked rotation removes its row and column), `kinetic_energy()` adds
`ωᵀ Jₙ ω`, `rotary_inertia()` and the dump field `rotary_inertia` hold the
blocks, `lumped_mass()` their diagonal.

`LumpedRotaryInertia::IsotropicMax` adds `max(J_t, J_b)` to all three
rotations of both nodes: diagonal and objective, for users who need a
diagonal mass. Torsional frequencies are then low by about
`1 − √(J_t/J_b)` on coarse elements (−82.5 %, −64.8 %, −29.4 % on the
shaft above); on fine elements bending converges to the Rayleigh-beam value
(offset −4.4e-4).

Stable time step: the two rules give the same step on coarse straight
members (`L > h* = √(39 I_p/A)`, `4.42 r`), where the axial wave governs.
`Block3x3`'s step is never longer than `IsotropicMax`'s and is shorter when
`h < h*` (1.39× and 2.41× at 24 and 48 elements of the reference shaft), so
a time step chosen with the diagonal rule can be unstable with the default.

Every free DOF needs a positive, finite entry, and with `Block3x3` in 3-D
every node's free rotary sub-block must be numerically positive definite
(each `LDLᵀ` pivot above `ROTARY_BLOCK_DEFINITENESS` = 1e-12 times the
block's largest diagonal entry, a conditioning guard, not a physical
criterion). Collinear beams without polar moment (`J_t = 0`) are refused
with `Error::Numerical` unless their axis rotation is blocked;
`IsotropicMax` accepts them by giving that rotation `J_b`. Measurements:
`docs/NUMERICAL_VALIDATION.md`, structural section.

## Solvers, DOF managers and time integration

### UD-202: why the increment is kept

`"external work new"` (`SolidMechanicsModel::assembleResidual` adds
`getExternalWorkIncrement()` once per displacement release, that is after
the predictor and after every corrector, `solid_mechanics_model.cc:245-249,
332-335`) and the split-node contact's slip and work
(`NTNContactSolverCallback::assembleResidual`, per residual assembly) add
the increment once per corrector inside a Newton loop, and the last
correction is exactly the change since the previous accumulation. Setting
`u − u_previous` for every type would re-add the earlier corrections at
each iteration and over-count multi-iteration steps, so it corrects no
reader. Neither definition captures the predictor's move (the predictor
does not update the increment), so in implicit dynamics `"external work
new"` is not the step's work under either; a per-step work or slip needs
those readers changed. `"external work"` reads the increment only in
static analyses (velocities otherwise, `:576-580`); the phase-field
coupler's and the contact model's increment arrays are not read. As the
Newmark predictor moves `u`, even a one-iteration implicit-dynamic step's
increment is the correction from the predicted state.

### UD-287: convergence orders of the corrected scheme

Against the exact semi-discrete solution, backward Euler is first order
from any initial rate. The corrected trapezoidal rule 1 (Crank–Nicolson)
is second order from a consistent initial rate `M u̇⁰ = f − K u⁰` and first
order from the default `u̇⁰ = 0`, since its first step is then half a
backward-Euler step; every upstream run starts from 0. The port extension
`DiffusionModel::set_consistent_initial_rate` sets the consistent rate
(the default is unchanged). With upstream's predictor neither scheme
converges. Under `Δt ∝ h²` the spatial-temporal rate is at least 1.8.

### UD-299: complex division across platforms

The dense probe differs between platforms in 45 complex-division and 56
eigen-decomposition records of 2,250. On 2·10⁷ random inputs, 141,017
results differ from the aarch64 model and 126 from its unfused variant;
those 126 differ only in the sign of a zero (real part in 66, imaginary
part in 60), every one with `|c|` or `|d|` equal to
`0x1.fffffffffffffp+970`, just below the model's 2⁹⁷¹ upscaling guard. With
the guard at `< 0x1.fffffffffffffp+970`, the unfused model leaves 0 of
2·10⁷ + 104,976 inputs different. Baseline x86-64 has no FMA instruction,
an ISA fact; libgcc's code was not inspected. The x86_64 goldens hold 188
records (101 of class X4, 87 of class X3).

### UD-1060: the periodic distribution case

`periodic_info_to_send_per_proc` is a `std::vector<Array<Idx>>` grown by
`emplace_back` inside the rank loop; each rank's entry is filled (and, for
a non-root rank, handed to `asyncSend`) before the next `emplace_back`. A
reallocation relocates the earlier entries with `Array`'s move (`noexcept`,
so `std::vector` moves rather than copies), which leaves the moved-from
entry owning its buffer; destroying it frees the buffer that the relocated
entry still uses.

Which entries are relocated depends on the standard library's growth
policy, which the standard does not fix; it was observed black-box with
the oracle's g++ 14.2.0 (Debian 14.2.0-19): capacities 1, 2, 4, 4, 8, 8,
8, 8, so the entries 0 to 2^k − 1 are relocated, with 2^k the largest
power of two below the rank count (rank 0 at 2 ranks, ranks 0 to 1 at 3 to
4 ranks, 0 to 3 at 5 to 8). Another compiler or standard library needs a
new observation.

When a relocated entry holds periodic pairs (its rank holds a periodic
slave node in a local or ghost element), the root reads its own pairs from
freed memory (`fillPeriodicPairs`, line 582) and the vector's destructor
frees the buffer again (glibc "double free or corruption", UD-655). An
entry relocated twice (entry 0 with 3 to 4 ranks) is freed at each later
relocation, so if rank 0 holds slaves the double free happens inside the
loop (line 547); a relocated non-root entry has already been handed to
`asyncSend` (line 565), so MPI may read freed memory. An empty entry holds
`nullptr` and is harmless.

Valgrind memcheck of the parallel probe's `distribution_square_periodic`
(2 ranks) shows invalid 4-byte reads in `fillPeriodicPairs`
(`node_info_per_processor.cc:214`) of a 16,008-byte block freed by
`~Array` in the reallocation at line 547 and allocated by
`Array::push_back` at line 554, then "Invalid free" from `~vector` at line
614. The oracle cases `parallel/pbd_strip_{scotch,mesh_data}/np{1,2,4}`
record the precondition (`ud1060/slave_holders`,
`ud1060/upstream_memory_safe`); a 24 × 4 strip with Scotch seed 1234
satisfies it at 2 and 4 parts, a 16 × 3 strip does not for seeds 1234 to
1293. These recorded inputs are memcheck-clean (valgrind 3.24.0, 2 and 4
ranks). The RCB partition of `periodic_distribution_invariants` violates
the precondition at 2 and 4 ranks, so there the port is checked without an
oracle.

### UD-499: MPI finalisation at exit

The hook is registered once, when `initialize` installs an MPI
communicator (`runtime::static_communicator()`: with feature `mpi` a
duplicate of `MPI_COMM_WORLD`). It releases the runtime's communicator and
completes the finalisation on MPI's main thread: models, native backends,
requests, communicators, then `MPI_Finalize`. Like upstream after an
uncaught failure (an uncaught exception ends in `std::terminate` without
static destructors), the hook does nothing after a panic since
`initialize` or, on glibc Linux, a non-zero exit status. It makes no MPI
call once MPI is finalised, never blocks, and uses a thread key that does
not need thread-local storage (`std::thread::current()` aborts inside
`atexit` handlers on macOS).

Deliberate differences (decision D-067): a panic that is caught and handled
still disables the hook, so the job ends with a non-zero status unless the
program calls `runtime::finalize()`, whereas upstream finalises after a
caught exception; and upstream's static destructor finalises after
`exit(n)` with `n ≠ 0`, which the port does not on glibc. On targets
without `on_exit` (macOS, musl) a non-zero `exit` without a panic still
finalises MPI, as upstream does.

Limits: MPI stays unfinalised, and the launcher reports an abnormal exit,
if the process is terminated from a thread other than MPI's main thread
under `Single` or `Funneled` (upstream would call `MPI_Finalize` there,
which MPI forbids), or if objects holding a communicator are never dropped
(leaked, owned by a static, alive at `std::process::exit`).
`runtime::finalize()` is not a no-op: it finalises an akantu-owned MPI at
once, so a later `initialize` is refused with `Error::Communication`
naming UD-499 (MPI cannot be initialised twice; upstream's empty
`finalize` lets a later `initialize` reuse the static communicator).

### UD-1296: stale boundary engine after an insertion

The insertion gives every boundary element the row of its facet: the same
nodes in another order, with copies in place of doubled nodes. Point q of
the new parametrisation is the physical point the old one called π(q), and
it is weighted with J_q where J_π(q) is right. Measured with the test
`doubled_node_unequal_shares` without its engine refresh: 1.668e-3 of the
largest nodal load on a distorted hexahedral mesh at 1, 2 and 4 ranks;
1.33e-13 (`_segment_3`) and 4.07e-13 (`_triangle_6`) on upstream's planar
quadratic meshes, from the coordinate noise of the mesh files.

The stored normal keeps the orientation the row had when the engine was
created, so a rewrite that reverses a row's orientation does not reverse
the normal; upstream's orientation therefore depends on whether the
boundary engine was created before or after the first doubling.
`FeEngineRegistry::init_boundary` only creates a missing engine and is no
workaround.

A possible correction (a category B candidate, not implemented): on the
node event, permute each rewritten row's stored jacobians and normals by
matching quadrature points by position, keeping the old orientation; it is
neutral bit for bit for one-point elements and needs a switch that keeps
the stale data.

## Input and output

| UD-085 | `python/py_aka_array.hh:126` (`array_type_t({size, nbc}, src.data(), base)`) | NumPy views are raw pointers into `Array` storage: they dangle after a resize or reallocation (a cohesive insertion), a size-changing restore or the owner's destruction, and nothing orders NumPy accesses against native computation (Python callbacks may write arrays the solver is reading; NumPy may release the GIL). A call from another execution context of the same thread (a greenlet, a resumed generator) can also reach the objects of a running native call. | A | Implemented: views hold an export lease of a `SharedArray` and never dangle. A view stays attached until its allocation is retired (growth beyond capacity, any shrink, replacement, destruction) and then becomes stale but safe (reads return the values at retirement, writes no longer reach the array, output-buffer entry points raise). Native code touches exported values only inside a native section; a Python callback raises instead of running while native code holds array elements; another thread or execution context calling akantu during a running call gets `akantu.Exception`. Details below. | No switch | `akantu-python` `foreign_views.rs`, `py/exports.rs` unit test `cached_lease_is_refused_after_a_growth_within_capacity`; `python/tests/test_zero_copy_views.py` (including `test_v62_a3_other_thread_calls_during_a_callback_raise`) |

### UD-085: retained NumPy views

Growth within capacity keeps a view attached; it shows the old prefix, as
in upstream. Inside a Python callback of the running call that grew the
array, the array's getter raises instead of returning that prefix.
Restores of equal length (history) keep views attached; re-fetching
(`model.getDisplacement()`) returns an attached view.

The port cannot enforce one rule, which is documented: a NumPy operation
on an akantu view running in one Python thread with the GIL released must
not overlap an akantu call in another thread (NumPy's own rule for
concurrent mutation of one array); a copy (`np.array(view)`) avoids it.

Differences from upstream are observable only where upstream's behaviour
is undefined, plus: views are stale after a shrink (upstream: a pointer
into the freed tail); a callback is refused while native code holds array
elements (upstream: silent aliasing); another Python thread calling any
akantu method while a thread is inside an akantu call gets
`akantu.Exception` (category C; upstream runs it unsynchronised). Minor
differences: a view's `.base` is an `_ArrayLease` (upstream `None`;
`pickle` and `deepcopy` of `.base` raise `TypeError`), zero-size views
report C strides (upstream `(0, 0)`), and `getElementalDataUInt` on an
`Int` dataset raises `akantu.Exception` (upstream `RuntimeError`).
